In México , the toltecs(not aztecs) they domesticated and modified a plant called "teocintle" to what we now know as corn or "maiz", they also included beand and pumpkim in the same cornfield, we call it "milpa". What's interesting is that each family had the right and obligation to grow their own "milpa" to cultivate their own food. They we're able to stack the food of an entire year in only 6 months, this gave them a lot of free time. With time this created groups of cientists dedicated to understand the world we don't see, they we're called "nahuales". ... ### **@daviddavidson1090** | 👍 26 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Jjqf9T59uY0&lc=UgxqYPPGcOt2vW8SpKR4AaABAg)

Farming before 12000 years ago was difficult because of the lower CO2 levels of the ice age. CO2 (which plants breathe) rose by ~50% ; crops became easier to grow. This is why farming as a lifestyle emerged in independent populations worldwide ~simultaneously (Mediterranean, China, America). 
"Domesticating" crops means when replanting, they selected for those with traits like bigger corn kernels. Over thousands of years this led to requiring less labor per pound of food.

### **@EMPPT** | 👍 16 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Jjqf9T59uY0&lc=Ugxcm0ao7hxJ-KAjP6l4AaABAg)

Topics discussed:

00:00 - Introduction (Why did humans shift from hunting and gathering to farming?)
00:40 - The Traditional Story (Agriculture as the foundation of civilization and progress.)
02:25 - Challenging the Traditional View (Questioning whether farming truly improved human life.)
03:08 - The Costs of Agriculture (Harder work, poorer health, and more disease.)
06:37 - Evidence of the Transition (If farming was worse, why did we do it?)
09:55 - Explaining the Transition (Coercion, war, elder respect, and religion as possible explanations)
13:22 - Evaluating the Theories (Religion emerges as the strongest candidate.)
18:07 - Göbekli Tepe: The First Temple (The world’s earliest known temple, built before farming.)
23:22 - The Birth of Religious Leadership (How faith and charisma inspired collective effort.)
25:26 - Architecture and Cosmology (Temples aligned with the stars and seasons.)
27:21 - Spiritual Experience and Psychedelics (Shamanic visions and altered states of consciousness.)
28:09 - Symbolism and Animal Imagery (Ritual art connecting humans, animals, and nature.)
31:28 - Jericho and the Natufian Culture (Sedentary hunter-gatherers and ancestor worship.)
34:13 - The Tower of Jericho (A cosmic monument aligned with mountain shadows.)
36:13 - Religion Leading to Agriculture (Farming developed to sustain ritual gatherings.)
37:57 - The Birth of Social Complexity (From ritual cooperation to hierarchy and specialization.)
39:57 - Cognitive Revolution and Belief Systems (Shared myths and the power of imagination.)
41:47 - The Power of Shared Myths (Belief enabling large-scale cooperation.)
43:10 - Gains and Losses of Agriculture (Civilization’s benefits came with heavy costs.)
45:27 - Was It Worth It? (Progress or a spiritual trap?)
47:22 - Rethinking Human Progress (Faith and imagination, the true roots of civilization.)

### **@JeffreyWang-hh4ss** | 👍 3 likes

I have always thought that the agricultural tribes appeared by accident, largely due to there were a period of time for a certain latitude in northern hemisphere made it very easy to grow wheat. So those tribes were able to settle with very low cost and expanded to a larger population very quickly. 

Then the advantage of a larger population became clear, especially the ability of forming a bigger army that could defeat the enemies they couldn’t before, probably kill off other smaller tribes who competed for food. So in a longer run these agricultural tribes just survived more and advanced technologically or religiously, giving them ability to grow into even bigger population.

Of course it was a gradual process which took like tens of thousands of years, something like a natural selection process but on a societal level. Just like there are still some hunter and gathering tribes in the world but they are close to extinction.

There are a few pieces of misinformation/unsubstantiated information in this video, mostly others have pointed out, but one I’ll add is to refute the claim that ancient humans were not violent, or that there is no evidence of violence. We have found remnants of human skulls that we can determine have been scalped dating back well over 100,000 years. It is likely even early proto-humans engaged in violence. I haven’t seen evidence to know all of the cultural reasons that violence could have happened or how organized/unorganized the violence was, but we have certainly always been as capable of violence as chimps.

The word paradigm is not an English word (as you said) but Greek, it is "παράδειγμα -paradigma" from the noun "δείγμα" of the verb "δείχνω" (which means I show, notice, indicate) with the adverb "παρα-" which means "here, next to". Παράδειγμα-paradigm in greek means something that I show here as an example, a case study. Of course, in English it has acquired a more sophisticated meaning.

### **@theoldesttrees** | 👍 1 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Jjqf9T59uY0&lc=UgyJieA52pl5gVp1f-54AaABAg)

Regarding Gobekle Tepe and other ancient stone structures, stellar observatories seems to be the most reasonable explanation. But there is evidence that they are also recording the cyclical nature of our planet's encounters with asteroids, including depictions of constellation configuration (the animals) during the period of time that the asteroids might appear.
There is actually a mindblowing amount of art throughout human history that seems to be depictions of extraterrestrial events (not "aliens"). The depictions of birds with long tails are thought to be a representation of comets flying across the sky.
It's not a stretch to assume that humans trying to figure out how to stock up on food during times of scarcity, like the Younger Dryas, would be learning how to cultivate and preserve food pretty quickly, especially after hundreds of thousands of years of foraging.
One of the many misunderstandings by modern "civilized" humans is that indigenous hunter gatherers do actively cultivate wild cultivars. The entire American continent was a carefully cultivated garden over several millenia, according to their own lore.

### **@RômulodeBarros**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Jjqf9T59uY0&lc=UgzY_VKA6IUm5i9wX0J4AaABAg)

That’s so cool! Since I was in school back in the 90s and early 2000s, I completely missed out on this whole paradigm shift. From what I’ve gathered, the 'religion theory' only really started blowing up in the 2000s.

The craziest part: Göbekli Tepe was actually found way back in '63 by a team from Istanbul and Chicago, but they totally blew it off. The archaeologists at the time saw the tops of the limestone pillars and just figured they were gravestones from some medieval cemetery.

It wasn't until 1994 that this German archaeologist, Klaus Schmidt, checked the place out and immediately clocked that the flint chips and the shape of the pillars meant it was way older—like, Stone Age old. That’s when the digging and the studies really kicked off, eventually making the religion theory a huge deal.

### **@MartinThau** | 👍 44 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Jjqf9T59uY0&lc=UgzXbwBFbCamOLU54Lp4AaABAg)

The transition to agriculture and settled life was likely driven primarily by ecological necessity: as the climate changed at the end of the last Ice Age, many large game species disappeared or became scarce, making hunting increasingly unreliable as a food source. In response, human groups began to cultivate plants, leading gradually to the development of farming and permanent settlements. While some scholars emphasize the role of religion in this transition—arguing that shared rituals and beliefs helped solidify communal life—the more fundamental cause seems to have been the need to secure a stable calorie supply. It is likely that both factors interacted: ecological pressure pushed people towards farming, while religious and cultural developments emerged as a way to give meaning and cohesion to the new way of life.

>   
> My take is that the sedentary life gave the tribe more time to think, try to explain the sun/moon/seasons, cultivate art and develop abstract ideas - this led to religion and communal rituals.  
>   
> You also assert they "are not as advanced as we are". Again I disagree, we are not different to humans 20,000+ years ago. They may not have had writing but surely some verbal language and means to transmit cultural and technical ideas to each other.  
>   
> Respectfully, you should read into this topic more. You will then see the comments by @MartinThau more clearly.

Seems pretty obvious to me that the main advantage of agriculture is the ability to create and store a surplus of food. Even herding has this advantage over hunter gatherers. Having a surplus makes you resistant to sudden lean times. Hunter gatherers probably wasted tons of food that they couldn't carry with them. Cultural evolution doesn't care that their life was easier and more pleasant in good times, it cares whether they were able to stay alive through lean times.

> ​@Mel_Flips_It  With respect, there's a difference between hunting for recreation vs hunting as your primary food source. Almost all true hunter gatherers are nomadic, because it requires following migrating herds. It doesn't matter if you can preserve and store meat - if you have to leave it behind to follow your migrating prey, it does you no good, especially in a time before manufactured cans and jars. Hence my original point, that hunter-gatherers would not have access to a large store of food compared to agriculturalists, so they would be more vulnerable to food shocks.  
>   
> Also, you mention harvesting, which is a part of sedentary agriculture - not hunter-gathering. So I'm honestly not even sure what argument you're even making now tbh. The discussion we're having here is about the relative merits of hunter-gathering lifestyles compared to settled/agricultural/farming lifestyles. You farm for your food, so you are in the second category. Nobody here is talking about the merits of a generic rural lifestyle vs a generic urban lifestyle. Yes, a country boy can survive. Very cool. But every single country boy in the English speaking world is dependent on sedentary agriculture, we don't have hunter-gatherers anymore.

These myths were from all corners of the globe. So agriculture rose as a "backup" to hunting. Meat was always the staple of the human diet, until it wasn't. However, even as agriculture expanded, meat remained the primary staple.

### **@dbar8640** | 👍 1 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Jjqf9T59uY0&lc=UgwW5TY_IKMeig5C3vp4AaABAg)

People often assume that hunter-gatherers automatically “evolved” into agricultural societies, but history is not that linear.



What if hunter-gatherers didn’t evolve into farmers — but instead represented a different, equally valid way of organizing society?



Even today, highly technological civilizations coexist alongside hunter-gatherer communities, such as some tribes in the Amazon. Their existence doesn’t mean they are “behind” us — it means different lifestyles can successfully adapt to different environments.



Some groups adopted it because it made sense for their conditions; others did not, because mobility and ecological knowledge were more sustainable for them.



So when we look back 10,000 years, it is entirely plausible that multiple forms of social organization coexisted — including complex or advanced societies alongside hunter-gatherer groups.

15:00 In humans coercion is not just physical, that can be overtrown easily as said in the previous sentences, but it can be also social/psychological. If somebody can but himself in a position of socio-psychological power then coercion can be also applied and that is the case for example in early mesopotamia in the context of priest-king ruling, where they put themselves in the position of being the ones closer to the gods so in order to gain the gods favour people would follow the priest-king lead. As primates have an alpha strong individual that governs over resources allegedly protecting from external treats, a figure just like the the priest-king could play the role of the intermediary between regular people and the gods and also offering a road to their favour and therefore protection. This would fall under the religion umbrella but coercion is still a valuable framework to take into consideration.

### **@Hi5Aussie**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Jjqf9T59uY0&lc=UgwS3IReH4ZqbsrcQ2l4AaABAg)

Great lectures, profesor. I'm at the video #8 and enjoying it so far. Thanks for the interesting overview. A few quick points of criticism to this one first: Modern genetics show we are actually closer to chimpanzees than bonobos. Also, the idea that there is no evidence for early warfare isn't quite right and Jebel Sahaba from over 13OOO years ago is an example. Many of the interpretations of Jericho and the "religion-first" theory for agriculture are presented here as consensus, but there is actually still a lot of debate on those topics. Factors like climate change and population pressure may be more important. For the Tower of Jericho still widely held view is that the tower and walls associated with it were defensive fortifications. 
Just some food for thought!
Cheers

"Scientists said Wednesday they had found the oldest evidence of human warfare, fossils of a band of people massacred by a troop of attackers with weapons including arrows, clubs and stone blades on the shores of a lagoon in Kenya about 10,000 years ago.



The remains of 27 people from a Stone Age hunter-gatherer culture were unearthed at a site called Nataruk roughly 20 miles (30 km) west of Lake Turkana in northern Kenya.



One man's skeleton was found with a sharp blade made of a volcanic glass called obsidian still embedded in his skull.



Another man had wounds from two blows to the head apparently with a club, crushing his skull. A woman in the last stages of pregnancy appeared to have been bound by her hands and feet.



Victims also had projectile wounds to the neck and broken skulls, hands, knees and ribs."

### **@kaedatiger**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Jjqf9T59uY0&lc=Ugx_hja1A770yqO6Tux4AaABAg)

I think it was yeast. Before farming, people knew how to grind up flour and mix it with water and cook it. If someone left it out for a few hours before cooking it, it could have risen from the yeast and turned into fluffy bread. Alternately, if you add more water and leave it out for a few days, you get beer. I doubt it took very long after farming for people to figure out that vinegar is an excellent food preservative as well. As far as I know, hunter-gatherers don't eat yeast, so it may have been yeast that enslaved humanity and they saw the potential when they found the bread basket of the world. If I'm not mistaken, hunter-gatherers use dehydration as their food preservation method, whereas farmers use pickling.

### **@Dioxine-t2s**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Jjqf9T59uY0&lc=UgxgHGqo66h2QX0UkVR4AaABAg)

Beer theory (supplementary, not mutually exclusive, with Religion theory):
1. Very good conditions in some places make wild grain useful to gather and mill in some quantity.
2. Beer gets invented.
3. People do not want to leave sedentary lifestyle allowing for beer.
4. Younger Drias comes, sharp decline in climate
5. Because of 3), people instead of leaving, try to cultivate the grain they were only gathering so far.
6. Some critical threshold of dependency on grain is passed (perhaps to the tune of 20% farming, 80% hunting, or so), the proto-farmers gain numerical advantage over hunters, and it snowballs from there.

### **@BeefBronson** | 👍 1 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Jjqf9T59uY0&lc=Ugyykz-e6BuK0cXlFFx4AaABAg)

Phenomenal lecture, professor! In the interest of creating a more level pedagogy, I have created a synthesis summarizing the lecture and my main takeaways from it. I plan to comment a similar synthesis in the comments after every lecture for others to review and add thoughts + critiques :)

The traditional paradigm of the transition of early humans from hunter-gatherer societies to agriculture, where food production was the primary driving factor, is desirable for imperialist historians: it is ego-driven and apathetic to other forms of life (anthrocentric), ignores inconvenient truths of other paradigms, justifies expansionist beliefs (the continual need to acquire arable land), and obscures the secular nature of modern society as opposed to early human civilization. However, it's far more likely that religion was the primary driver of both a sedentary lifestyle and agriculture in early humans. Some early human cultures were aware of agriculture as an option and still chose to be hunter-gatherers; they were aware that transitioning to agriculture had associated risks and required religion to fully buy in. Animal veneration in early religion was indicative of the shame early humans felt about hunting and killing; agriculture and settlement both provided a desirable alternative. Religion provided not only ritual/routine, structure, community, and opportunities for mating, but also a holistic understanding of reality where everything could be explained by and incorporated into religion. This totalizing shift allowed charismatic religious leaders, who "proved" their beliefs with "magic," to leverage religious belief for the development of technology and agriculture despite peoples' reservations. It's inconvenient to modern historians that religious and scientific development were provably intertwined in early human culture as a holistic search for meaning; modern science lays just as much claim to objectivity and being "civilized" as early human science, we merely have the benefit of several thousand years of accumulated knowledge. Centering religion as a universal and potent driver of the development of civilization writ large provides an alternative framework to imperialist conceptions of history, and elucidates the ways in which religion can manipulate large populations. It is therefore critical to understand that religion and human civilization are in a symbiotic relationship with one-another--they are inseparable.

The oldest known calendar of the Turks is the 12-Animal Turkic Calendar.



Each year is represented by an animal: rat, ox, tiger, rabbit, dragon, snake, horse, sheep, monkey, rooster, dog, pig.



It operates on a 12-year cycle, with each year believed to carry a unique energy or character.



It is solar-based rather than lunar.

### **@jeanmarceltech**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Jjqf9T59uY0&lc=UgwsC1We00t6qCEgLFB4AaABAg)

Follow all my notes from this Lecture: Civilization #1: Explaining Humanity's Transition to Agriculture
URL: https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Jjqf9T59uY0&ab_channel=PredictiveHistory

* Let us consider the question: Why did humanity transition from a hunter-gatherer society to an agricultural one?
* For most of our history, humans lived in small groups of roughly 20 to 50 individuals, moving from place to place in search of food.
* Then came a profound turning point — the discovery and development of farming.
* Agriculture allowed humans to gain control over food production and to anticipate resources, creating surpluses.
* These surpluses meant that not everyone needed to work in food production. This opened the way for new roles to emerge: leaders, politicians, artists, priests, writers, and others.
* Over time, small farming communities expanded, evolving from villages into towns and eventually into cities
* Over the course of this historical transformation, humanity laid the foundations of what we now call modernity.
* The transition from a hunter-gatherer lifestyle to agriculture has traditionally been regarded as the pivotal step toward this modern world.
* This has long been the dominant narrative in our understanding of history.
* Yet, the evidence tells a different story. In fact, the more archaeological and anthropological data we collect, the more it becomes clear that this traditional view is not accurate.
* From a modern perspective, the shift from hunting and gathering to farming was, in many respects, an unwise decision, for several reasons:

> **@jeanmarceltech**  
> [View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Jjqf9T59uY0&lc=UgwsC1We00t6qCEgLFB4AaABAg.ALgIgZeT1EqALgIi-iWVLq)  
>
> *** As hunter-gatherers, food could be found in abundance across the landscape; as farmers, humans had to produce their own food, often through backbreaking and time-consuming labor.  
> *** As the saying goes, “We did not domesticate wheat — wheat domesticated us.”  
> *** Agriculture required more labor, which encouraged larger families to work the fields — leading to overpopulation and the pressures that come with it.  
> *** Intensive farming gradually exhausts the soil, rendering land infertile and reducing its long-term productivity.  
> * Skeletal evidence from early agricultural communities reveals that hunter-gatherers were generally taller than farmers, suggesting that their diet provided superior nutrition.  
> * Data also indicates that farmers faced a higher risk of early death compared to hunter-gatherers, largely due to the spread of infectious diseases in settled communities.  
> * Even today, the exact reasons for humanity’s transition to agriculture remain uncertain.  

> **@jeanmarceltech**  
> [View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Jjqf9T59uY0&lc=UgwsC1We00t6qCEgLFB4AaABAg.ALgIgZeT1EqALgIj_R-zT5)  
>
> * Evidence can be explored through four main fields of study: archaeology, anthropology, psychology and neuroscience, and primatology.  
> * Remarkably, 99% of our DNA is shared with chimpanzees, and both chimpanzees and bonobos are among our closest genetic relatives.  
> * One prominent theory about the transition to agriculture is known as the Coercion Theory.  
> * It suggests that a small group of leaders, unwilling to work themselves, compelled others to labor on their behalf.  
> * Evidence supporting this idea is often drawn from the behavior of gorillas.  
> * In gorilla groups, there is typically a dominant alpha male — physically larger than the others — who uses his status to make the rest of the group perform the necessary work.  
> * This alpha male claims the majority of resources for himself, including food and access to females.  

> **@jeanmarceltech**  
> [View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Jjqf9T59uY0&lc=UgwsC1We00t6qCEgLFB4AaABAg.ALgIgZeT1EqALgIl3ZFfvw)  
>
> * The second theory is known as the War Theory.  
> * It proposes that farming communities offered greater protection from conflict than a nomadic life in the forest.  
> * Evidence for this idea comes from the behavior of chimpanzees.  
> * Chimpanzees display patterns of territorial violence, sometimes engaging in organized and lethal raids to seize resources.  
> * Observing our close relatives behave in this way suggests that early human groups may also have fought over land.  
> * Agriculture could have emerged as a strategic response to this threat, enabling communities to secure and defend their food supply within well-protected areas.  
> * The third theory is known as the Respect for Elders Theory.  
> * Life as a hunter-gatherer could be especially difficult for the elderly, who might be unable to keep pace with the group or gather sufficient food. In many cases, older members may have been left behind, unable to survive on their own.  
> * This theory proposes that humans began farming, in part, to care for aging members of the community. Agriculture allowed people to remain in one location and produce a stable food supply, making it possible to support elders.  
> * Caring for the elderly would have strengthened social bonds, preserved valuable knowledge, and enhanced the community’s long-term success.  
> * Evidence for this view lies in the near-universal cultural tendency to respect and care for elders.  
> * The fourth theory is the Religion Theory.  

> **@jeanmarceltech**  
> [View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Jjqf9T59uY0&lc=UgwsC1We00t6qCEgLFB4AaABAg.ALgIgZeT1EqALgImoPAe3r)  
>
> * It suggests that agriculture may have arisen to support religious or spiritual practices.  
> * Archaeological evidence shows that some of the earliest farming sites are located near ancient temples or ritual spaces. Food might have been cultivated to feed priests, to offer to deities, or to sustain large gatherings for ceremonies.  
> * Farming enabled permanent settlement, which in turn allowed for the construction of enduring religious centers, around which communities could gather and grow.  
> * Many scholars today see religion as a likely driving force — or at least a significant catalyst — behind the emergence of agriculture.  
> * Let us now consider why religion stands out as the most convincing explanation for humanity’s transition to agriculture.  
> * If we examine the first theory — Coercion — it does not fit well with human biology or social dynamics. In gorillas, for example, alpha males are much larger than females, giving them the physical advantage to dominate the group and enforce obedience. Humans, however, are roughly similar in size across genders, and even a physically stronger individual could be resisted if others cooperated. This makes coercion far more difficult to sustain in human communities.  
> * The second theory — War — also falls short. While chimpanzees, one of our close relatives, can display violent and territorial behavior, bonobos — who are even more closely related to us genetically — are largely peaceful and avoid lethal conflict. Archaeological evidence also fails to support widespread violence in early human societies: we find no significant weaponry in burial sites, nor signs of large-scale warfare between groups.  
> * The third theory — Respect for Elders — is more plausible, but still has weaknesses. In hunter-gatherer cultures, death was likely seen as a natural part of the life cycle, much like the changing seasons. Elders may have been respected, but there is little evidence that prolonging life at all costs was a driving cultural priority. The acceptance of death as part of nature could have reduced the motivation to radically alter the way of life simply to support the elderly.  
> * That leaves Religion as the most compelling explanation. The earliest farming sites often appear near ritual centers, suggesting that agriculture may have begun as a way to sustain religious practices — feeding priests, producing offerings, and supporting large ceremonial gatherings. Permanent settlements enabled the construction of temples and sacred spaces, around which communities could organize. Shared spiritual beliefs would have bound people together, creating a strong cultural and social foundation for the agricultural revolution.  
> * To strengthen the religion hypothesis, we can examine three key archaeological sites: Göbekli Tepe, Jericho, and Çatalhöyük — all discovered or extensively studied in the past few decades.  

> **@jeanmarceltech**  
> [View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Jjqf9T59uY0&lc=UgwsC1We00t6qCEgLFB4AaABAg.ALgIgZeT1EqALgIoVIlia7)  
>
> * Göbekli Tepe — Located in Upper Mesopotamia, in present-day Turkey, this Neolithic site was inhabited from roughly 9500 BCE to 8000 BCE, during the Pre-Pottery Neolithic period. The site features massive T-shaped stone pillars, many carved with images of animals, which are widely interpreted as symbolic representations of human figures. The arrangement of these pillars suggests a ritual or ceremonial purpose, making Göbekli Tepe the world’s oldest known temple complex. Its construction predates the advent of agriculture in the region, hinting that religious activity may have preceded — and possibly inspired — the development of farming.  
> * Jericho — Considered one of the oldest continuously inhabited cities in the world, Jericho also boasts the oldest known defensive wall. Archaeologists have identified over 20 successive layers of settlement here, the earliest dating to around 9000 BCE. While Jericho later became a fortified urban center, its early phases reveal a community that could sustain itself in one location — a shift possibly tied to organized social and ritual activities.  
> * Çatalhöyük — Located in southern Anatolia, this large Neolithic and Chalcolithic proto-city thrived from approximately 7500 BCE to 5600 BCE, reaching its peak around 7000 BCE. The settlement shows evidence of complex social organization and symbolic art integrated into domestic spaces. Wall paintings, figurines, and shrines within homes indicate that spiritual beliefs were deeply embedded in daily life, reinforcing the idea that religion played a central role in early urban and agricultural development.  
> * Religious sites were often led by shamans—charismatic leaders who inspired devotion and attracted followers.  
> * It is possible that shamans constructed these monumental places as centers where people could gather to learn about religious beliefs and meet others. Over time, some visitors might have chosen to settle nearby, eventually building homes and forming communities. This is one plausible theory.  
> * The design of these temples holds cosmological significance; their structures often align with celestial bodies such as the sun and stars. Almost like an ancient clock, these alignments suggest an intention to connect the community with the wider universe.  
> * For those early builders, this was less about “religion” as we understand it today and more about a form of proto-science—a way to observe, understand, and harmonize with the natural world.  
> * At Göbekli Tepe, the T-shaped pillars are adorned with carvings of animals depicted in motion, often in hunting scenes. One theory to explain this imagery is that early humans did not see themselves as separate from the animal world but as part of a larger, interconnected cosmos.  
> * In early belief systems, it was thought that if you killed an animal without first asking for its forgiveness, its spirit might come back to haunt you.  

> **@jeanmarceltech**  
> [View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Jjqf9T59uY0&lc=UgwsC1We00t6qCEgLFB4AaABAg.ALgIgZeT1EqALgIpmbbih7)  
>
> * One way to seek forgiveness was by offering a tribute to the animal’s spirit.  
> * Another interpretation is that these animals—often depicted as skilled hunters—were revered for their strength, speed, and wisdom. Through ceremonies, people sought to channel these qualities for themselves.  
> * This represents one of the earliest forms of religious practice known to us.  
> * Now, let us turn our attention to Jericho.  
> * Jericho was part of the Natufian culture, an archaeological culture that thrived in the Near East—covering areas of modern-day Israel, Palestine, Jordan, Lebanon, and Syria—between approximately 15,000 and 11,500 years ago.  
> * The Natufian culture is remarkable for three key characteristics:  

> **@jeanmarceltech**  
> [View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Jjqf9T59uY0&lc=UgwsC1We00t6qCEgLFB4AaABAg.ALgIgZeT1EqALgIrZhy5Q3)  
>
> *** They were largely sedentary, living in permanent settlements while continuing to hunt gazelles for food.  
> *** They had begun domesticating crops, demonstrating the capacity for farming, yet interestingly, they often chose not to fully adopt agriculture.  
> *** They practiced what is known as the “cult of the skull,” likely indicating early forms of ancestor worship. Archaeologists have discovered human skulls carefully preserved within Natufian houses, suggesting a spiritual reverence for their forebears.  
> * The Natufian people’s understanding of the world was remarkably sophisticated—comparable in complexity to our own ways of interpreting life and nature.  
> * Now, let us turn to Çatalhöyük, dating back to around 7500 BCE. This was a vast settlement, possibly home to as many as 8,000 inhabitants—a substantial size for its time.  
> * Despite its large population, Çatalhöyük lacked a centralized place of worship or formal government.  
> * Interestingly, all the houses were quite similar in size and design, suggesting a relatively egalitarian society.  
> * Another fascinating aspect is that each house contained its own temple or shrine room, serving as a private space for worship and religious practice within the home.  
> * In Çatalhöyük, religion was woven into every aspect of daily life—present in every space and moment, from birth to death.  
> * Their religious beliefs were highly sophisticated, serving as a framework to explain the world and human experience.  
> * Many houses contain wall paintings depicting groups of people hunting bears and deer.  
> * Some scholars propose an alternative interpretation: rather than hunting scenes, these images may represent people dancing or celebrating alongside the animals, highlighting a deep connection and harmony with nature.  
> * Some theories suggest that the inhabitants of Çatalhöyük worshipped a Mother Goddess, emphasizing the vital importance of birth as the means to renew and sustain their society.  
> * Other interpretations propose that the bull symbolized energy and vitality, embodying masculine power and strength.  
> * Taken together, these observations highlight the profound role that religion played in the lives of early sedentary communities, influencing their decision to settle and farm.

### **@drcemdede**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Jjqf9T59uY0&lc=Ugw7pCHJVua99zoVqGZ4AaABAg)

Thank you for the lecture. 
However, I find the “rockstar shamans” interpretation inadequate for explaining the scale, intentionality, and coherence of Göbekli Tepe’s architecture. The site’s organizational complexity—across multiple enclosures, repeated iconography, and precise stonework—reflects a level of social coordination and symbolic thought that exceeds what the “charismatic shaman” model can satisfactorily account for.

The persistent astronomical motifs strengthen this critique. Many of the pillars appear aligned with specific celestial targets, and the symbolic clustering suggests sustained attention to particular constellations. Framing this solely as “religious belief” risks flattening what may have been a far more structured cosmological system. A more compelling interpretation is that these motifs represent a ritualized memory of a perceived celestial threat—an event significant enough to be encoded in architecture, ceremonies, and long-term cultural behavior as a means of preventing its recurrence.

The animal carvings do not resemble casual representations, celebrations of fauna, or appeasement rituals. They appear symbolic and patterned—potentially encoding seasonal markers, clan totems, mythic narratives, or even constellational analogues. Several theories propose that the animals correspond to star groups, astronomical cycles, or shared cultural myths tied to environmental upheavals. Maybe you should highlight these ideas as theories as well.

The “darkness” analogy you proposed for Nevalı Çori may, in fact, be far more dramatic than you described—perhaps even tied to a dawn or pre-dawn ritual cycle. As one of your students noted, no community deliberately darkens its own settlement without a compelling reason. If such a practice occurred, it was likely meant to recall something—something ominous, traumatic, or cosmologically troubling—rather than simply honoring stars or celebrating the night sky. This becomes even more compelling when we consider that the entire Taş Tepeler region appears to have been symbolically oriented toward the heavens. Göbekli Tepe is only one component of this much broader cultural landscape (Göbekli Tepe, Karahantepe, Nevalı Çori, Sefer Tepe, Harbetsuvan, Çakmaktepe, Sayburç, Ayanlar Höyük, Gürcütepe, Taşlı Tepe).

Hope you might consider these the next time you discuss these concepts. Thank you again!

### **@pushbeyondaverage**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Jjqf9T59uY0&lc=Ugw9aik046CaHsm13SN4AaABAg)

The Traditional Paradigm vs. Modern Understanding
The traditional view is that humanity transitioned to agriculture for a stable food source and a surplus, which led to the development of modern society [00:12].

The speaker explains that there is no evidence to support this view and that farming was a "stupid" choice for early humans [02:47].

The Disadvantages of Farming
More Work: Farmers worked six to seven hours a day, while hunter-gatherers worked as little as one hour a day [03:16].

Poorer Health: Archaeological evidence shows that hunter-gatherers were taller and had a more varied, nutritious diet [05:18].

Increased Disease and Mortality: Denser populations and unsanitary conditions led to the easier spread of diseases [05:57].

Theories for the Transition to Agriculture
The video notes that the reason for the transition is still uncertain, but researchers have developed theories based on four disciplines: Archaeology, Anthropology, Psychology/Neuroscience, and Primatology [07:21, 09:07].

The video debunks the theories of coercion, war, and respect for elders [10:16, 11:57].

The Leading Theory: Religion
The most plausible theory is that the transition to agriculture was driven by religion [12:47].

Göbekli Tepe (Turkey): An 11,000-year-old site with T-shaped pillars believed to be a religious place of worship built by hunter-gatherers [19:14].

Jericho (The Levant): This site belonged to the Natufian culture, which was sedentary but still hunted and gathered. The "Tower of Jericho" is now believed to be a cosmological religious monument [31:26]. They also practiced ancestor worship [33:41].

Çatalhöyük (Turkey): A large village from around 7500 BCE where each home served as a place of worship, indicating religion was a fundamental part of daily life [40:04]. They had a sophisticated religion involving a mother goddess and a bull [42:12].

One doubt I have about religion as the origin of sedentary societies is that religion seems to need sophisticated language as a prerequisite.

So, to communicate abstract ideas of other worlds, spirits, gods, you need a language right? Even to build those temples, you need to communicate between large groups of people to make sure they all pull the stone in the same direction.

But I also dont understand how a common language among large groups of people would have arisen prior to settled life. Kind of like a chicken and egg problem?

If people used to live in bands of 20 to 50 people going out and living in the forest, how did they have enough words to share a religion with other bands when they met at the religious place?

### **@TajOthman6**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Jjqf9T59uY0&lc=UgzgQxHtVBc7qZjOkvF4AaABAg)

47:00 "because birth is what rejuvenates your society" 

corollary from this: whatever those people thought would put society in a good place is then attributed to a god carrying the same epithets.

fast forward to ancient greece; basically i have to recall one of those stoic discourses that where the philosopher would tell the disciples that a god must certainly do some required deeds that would give him the right to retain those epithet.

in the first any 'greater good' you see (as natural as it can be) should be attributed back to the god. in the second any epithet predetermined for god will have him with certainty actualize what the epithet entails. they presupposed divine predicates and derived the reality of the divine from them.

a god of rejuvenation is worshiped for rejuvenating because he naturally rejuvinates, i.e. a god is named after what reality has already demonstrated.
in greece, a god must be just or he wouldn't be called that at all. 

it seems to me that the stoics inverted the causal logic of divinity sometime along their existence (or equally inherited it already inverted). in the first of course the practice sounds like the act of paying back a debt, the god exists and his good manifested already before you're born and throughout your life, and you have to pay it its due, (though i'm more interesting is the moment you do not pay the debt and there's the day of reckoning, where, historically, whatever natural cataclysm or social disaster that was supposed to be attributed to an angry god manifested as his will). In the second the relationship with god sounds participatory rather than transactional (because they believed that they shared their reasoning faculty (but not their will) with the divine representing the whole of it, and the people only the part, which allowed them to cultivate the same epithets that the gods actively retain (athena for example was their god of wisdom and people shared in her wisdom by bringing her totems and tokens etc, but the stoics were different in that they believed the only way they could share in it is by actually grooming it 'in accord with nature').

pls correct me if i'm wrong

### **@j.samuelwaters81**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Jjqf9T59uY0&lc=UgxGxMVt35bQxIJyMLl4AaABAg)

If it is the case that a) warfare requires weapons, and also that b) archeological evidence of weapons is sparse, we must conclude that c) early humans did not conduct warfare.

Sounds reasonable, but it does leave a lingering question:

If early humans didn't utilize weapons, how did they accomplish the hunting portion of being hunter-gatherers?

> **@Molly-jj2cy** | 👍 1 likes  
> [View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Jjqf9T59uY0&lc=UgxGxMVt35bQxIJyMLl4AaABAg.AJg4UoKnrzCAJnJ_vFUPeA)  
>
> An alternative argument ignoring evidence of weapons is that there's not a lot of good reasons to go to war in a hunter gatherer society.   
> Why have a gory dispute over territories or resources when the natural world at that time would have had more than enough resources to support multiple nomadic groups, and you could avoid a conflict by simply moving a few miles away.   
> With limited medical technologies and the social cost of a member of the community dying or becoming disabled in a group that small, war just isn't worth it.   
> There may have been personal grievances, or small conflicts over resources during lean seasons of course, but war follows resources throughout history, so it makes more sense for war, and not conflict, to come after farming than for war to come before farming!  
>   
> Early conflicts were definitely fought with hunting tools such as spears and arrows, of which there are plenty of evidence. Tools specifically designed for defence or murder would have been made in contexts where war and physical violence are common and widespread enough that devoting hours of time and valuable resources to a tool that kills people is worth it.  
>   
> Surplus of resources  > social constructs form around management and possession of resources > larger societies form > loyalty to a group larger than your immediate kin becomes a social force > interpersonal conflicts become inter-community wars > weapons become a necessity.  
>   
> I have no formal study in anthropology, just enjoy studying it casually in my spare time, so this is just my amateur argument as to why war would come after farming and not before 😅

> **@PredictiveHistory** | 👍 4 likes  
> [View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Jjqf9T59uY0&lc=Ugx_TphhU0y55kLrarl4AaABAg.AIftvyURxYxAIhHm5fWDNq)  
>
> @Golloo00-0 I am by no means an expert on neolithic religions, and quite honestly no one has these answers.  Most of my understanding comes from thinking about old religions (such as animism, Buddhism, Hinduism, and the Rosicrucians), and trying to imagine which parts might be shared with the peoples of Catal Huyuk and Gobekli Tepe.  I also make certain assumptions about them.  For example, that they focused on rituals as a way to structure their lives, and that they found meaning, purpose, and consolation in these rituals.  I also assumed that they didn't have a concept of linear time. (I really shouldn't have said "hundreds of years.")  The cult of the skull was a way to commemorate their beloved ancestor, to bind the community together, and to seek guidance from the spiritual world.  Looking up at the night sky and seeing so many stars they would not think that everyone would necessarily return -- maybe we're just one stop in an infinite journey across the cosmos?  I assume that they didn't have concepts such as the "finality of death" and dichotomies such as Heaven and Hell.  They imagined the world as balanced, harmonious, cyclical, and mysterious.  It was just important to focus on the here and now.  But I can't know anything for sure -- I'm just speculating.

### **@MarkArandjus**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Jjqf9T59uY0&lc=Ugx6TWm1XMP2CgJlrhZ4AaABAg)

I was a bit incredulous listening to this video for the first time, as I thought agriculture was an upgrade from hunting/gathering.
I checked research on the topic and yes, agriculture was a means to get more food and from smaller areas, which hallowed for bigger populations, but the quality of nutrition dropped.
From the Wikipedia page 'Neolithic Revolution':
_"Compared to foragers, Neolithic farmers' diets were higher in carbohydrates but lower in fibre, micronutrients, and protein. This led to an increase in the frequency of carious teeth and slower growth in childhood and increased body fat, and studies have consistently found that populations around the world became shorter after the transition to agriculture. This trend may have been exacerbated by the greater seasonality of farming diets and with it the increased risk of famine due to crop failure._

_Throughout the development of sedentary societies, disease spread more rapidly than it had during the time in which hunter-gatherer societies existed. Inadequate sanitary practices and the domestication of animals may explain the rise in deaths and sickness following the Neolithic Revolution, as diseases jumped from the animal to the human population."_

### **@AristoclesPlaton** | 👍 1 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Jjqf9T59uY0&lc=UgwnMqmWiusza7izA-x4AaABAg)

The hunter gatherers did not discover farming. The first wave of farmers to emerge from the Middle East and the Anatolian peninsula were the early proto-Indo-Europeans. They moved into the Western, Northern and Eastern European hunter gatherer lands, and conquered them, easily. The hunter gatherers were not match for the early settled peoples. We know from genetic studies that the village based mixed farming and seasonal hunter-gathering people killed the men of the nomadic hunter-gathering tribal people and married their woman (or stole them into sex-slavery). 

This happened twice, for later the true Indo-European expanded from the steppe (today's Ukraine and Southern Russia) again into Europe. And for a second time the men of Europe were slaughtered and the women stolen. 

There were three other places on Earth where agriculture was apparently independently discovered. One is the discovery of agriculture by the Bantu people of Western Sub-Saharan Africa, and the expansion of the Bantu people throughout the African continent. The other was the discover of agriculture in Asia in the Loose Plateau of northern China, and the spread of the Han people throughout the area that is China mainland today. The third was the discover of agriculture in the Americans and its spread from Central Americas (Yucatan) to both the north and south. Not one of these revolutions were as complete and as closely coupled with such a multitude of crops and diversity of animal husbandry practises, including the use of horses, as was the Middle-Eastern and European agricultural revolution. 

The point here, is this: It was not hunter-gatherers who discovered agriculture. Very tiny groups of people at various times and places discovered agriculture (perhaps as small as a few individuals initially), from where these technologies spread quickly. Hunter-gatherers were merely conquered, soundly defeated, mostly slaughtered (except for the best pick of the young women), and eventually eliminated. We may not have found weapons, as you claim, but the genetic evidence tells a story of genocidal violence. But, in any case, I contest your claim that we do not find weapons. The burial mounds of these late Neolithic, Calcolithic and early Bronze Age graves typically contain weapons: plenty of evidence there. And besides, the many mass graves that are known from these periods show vast numbers of traumatic deaths among young men. Just look at the many sites in Europe today, such as the Tollense Valley. 
https://youtu.be/aUhnU6sM18w?si=ueB5wxN5EqKzwvNM 

The agricultural revolution was not only about wheat. Agriculture in Europe included many crops, including vines (grapes) and trees (figs, apples, pears and stone fruits), vegetables, grains... And animal husbandry: goats, sheep and pigs in the first wave, with the addition of cattle and horses in the second wave. 

You draw sweeping conclusions, most of which are at best simplifications and in some cases wrong. For example, you raise three paradigmatic hypotheses to explain the formation of farming settlements: 
(1) Coercion 
(2) War
(3) Care for Elders
(4) Religion 
You dismiss the first three to pick the one that interests you (a literary man, not surprising) most. Leave aside for a moment the possibility that these are not the only, or even the main, theories to explain the formation of settlements (they are not), but your hasty dismissal of the first three on very dubious (though easily illustrated) grounds is poor scholarship. A more subtle thinker would examine how much and in what ways each of these contributed in certain ways to the formation of settled lifestyles in human pre-history. 

You are also careless with factual details. Only 98% of our DNA is common to other apes. Apes are not monkeys. We certainly do not share 99% of our DNA with monkeys.

### **@DarthMatusHolocron**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Jjqf9T59uY0&lc=Ugz-8x3i6EnFEOtWHgp4AaABAg)

34:12 Loving your classes, but for the sake of critical examination I feel I should point out that we don't know why they kept skulls in their homes. It clearly had a purpose, but that purpose could have been anything. Imagine discovering the pennant of the Boston Red Socks in ancient homes and trying to interpret its meaning. One possible explanation is worship, but there's any number of other explanations. 

Also, veneration does not equal worship. For example, say the skulls are the founding matriarch or patriarch of that particular family group, and the skulls are kept to remind the family of their heritage kind of like a coat of arms. This is certainly veneration of the ancestors, but it isn't worship. 

The truth is that its difficult to make assumptions about anything so far back and there is very little evidence overall. Often we can only project our modern understanding backwards, but this is rarely a good way to determine truth. 

The Paradox of Anthropology lol

It was the Anunnaki progenitors who genetically altered apes into humans and 'settled' them as agrarians in order to harness their labor in one spot...Also, as populations grew, it was no longer sustainable for everyone to hunt and gather...I believe both things are true...humans 'suddenly' appear around 200,000 years ago plus or minus...that kind of 'evolutionary' leap, while technically not impossible, is extremely unlikely...geneticists have discovered a gene-splice/alteration that cannot be explained by natural 'evolution'.  All the lore around the world tell of 'sky people' who came 'down' and interacted with 'humans' repeatedly...some of the interactions were benevolent and some destructive...humans could NOT have developed metallurgy without understanding all the different components and their ratios required to make 'steel'.
Minute amounts of various minerals and chemicals are used to modify steel and there were no microscopes to even look at the 
structure of the metals to see how they were modified by various additives.
Rock art and ancient sculptures, while it may be argued were influenced by psychedelics, ubiquitously show 'alien/humanoid' lifeforms.  They were certainly capable of at least crudely approximating the human forms they saw in 'others', but those depictions are relatively rare compared to the 'alien' depictions...the African tribe that knew of the Sirius star system including a 'hidden' star or planet is inexplicable without 'off world' knowledge...at the very least 'mind-travel' would have to be invoked, but the narratives include 'teachers' who 'from the sky came' and taught agriculture (GMO wheat-splice included), metallurgy, war planning, science, etc.  Did people work really hard to develop, for instance, 'spring-steel' for vehicle suspensions and other practical uses?  Sure, but that 'independence' didn't happen until the element tables and microscopes made it possible to map the material world at the molecular level.   It seems like we did it all by ourselves, but that wouldn't be possible to suddenly go from ape-to-man and then gradually, with 'great leaps' interspersed, become competent scientific discoverers and molders of matter...it just doesn't compute to believe we developed 'independently'!😎

### **@LostSoulAscension**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Jjqf9T59uY0&lc=UgxqfEblt18SAtN7tgR4AaABAg)

4:53 immediately I have to disagree with this premise that human's got a bad deal by switching to agrarian life.
Established settlements offered more protection and of course there was a trade off. But if the trade offs truly were a negative result, there wouldn't have been such a wide spread shift. The ability to grow communities and strength in numbers and defend against dangerous animals became more feasible back then, too. I think there's a bit of common sense here that 100's of years that led to this development there was enough trials and errors, and evolutionary testing that suggested the argrarian direction was ultimately the better route. Now, how that plays out into what we have today, was inevitable, and has potentially different implications for what we face today compared to back then. But if a hunter gatherer life is so much better, people could switch to it today and see the benefits and not need to go back. Being a hunter/gatherer means less stability like you said, not just in food availability but safety, and needing to move where the food is available, needing to compete against other animals that target the same foods. Human's cannot compete with animals in the wild without numbers and established settlements. 

And I'll end with forgive me, because I just realized that a hunter gatherer tribe can still have settlements, it became less nomadic over time, but that agrarian development became a logical notion. Maybe it's not what is better, but a mixture of everything that is important. But we are never to achieve the perfect balance and sustain that for extended periods of time. I still think some of what I said has some validity in mention. Thanks! 🙏

Hypothesis!! Not “theory”!’

A hypothesis is a tentative, testable prediction or proposed explanation for a specific phenomenon, while a theory is a comprehensive, well-substantiated explanation of a broader aspect of the natural world, backed by extensive verified data and tested hypotheses. A hypothesis is an educated guess that can be proven false, whereas a theory is a robust, widely accepted framework that has survived rigorous scientific testing and experimentation

The temple....built over hundreds even thousands of years. In that time....Hunter gatherers would have settled to farm. Which again....farming harder than hunting gathering? I dont think so. So over the many years of a fledgling agro village sets up hierarchies.  Hunting and gathering in no way just stops for agro. They are done together. It allows for larger civilizations.  Hunting doesnt take ' an hour". Hunters and gathering takes days or weeks. People get injured doing it. Then you have to skin it, or collect food that may not be close to where you set up, you also have to carve it up, transport it with many people, preserve it, etc. Not to mention dealing with hides.
Farming is difficult yes...but even a tiny bit of thinking as i have done destroys his theory. Again....over time things changed. Hence i say his " theories'are simplistic and reductive.  

Religion and god were indeed created by man. We know this. 
Early people began to realize that hey....i dont like my stuff being stolen, i dont like my house or village being burned, i dont like losing loved ones in battle for revenge or other things. So they created a social order or system which also evolved. Because they LEARNED that to have a peaceful and prosperous civilization they had to unite and follow sets of rules and guidelines.  Just like....you guessed it...hunting and gethering. 
Man still only thinks of survival. To believe otherwise is ignorant and foolish. A life with nothing else to do but find food and survive and procreate preceeds any type of rational thinking about ones self and humanity overall.  Especially lacking any type of tools, science or knowledge of the vast world left unexplored.

This lecture is misleading.

Mainstream view is that agriculture came first, driven by climate stability (Holocene), population pressure, and experimentation. Religion is usually seen as emerging alongside or after, then reinforcing sedentary life. Not causing it. There are minority theories (e.g. Göbekli Tepe) suggesting ritual may have encouraged settlement, but this is not the dominant explanation.

### **@camilavargas657**

Hmmm I’m feeling uncomfortable with this class, coming from the Geostrategy course. Last classes in Geostrategy had a lot of inacuracies regarding US american culture, and now this class has a level of confidence in what he is saying that makes me uncomfortable as an academic. Pre-history is all theoretic. We cannot know anything for certain from that period. It is a lie there is no evidence of war in those times, anyone who has gone to a history museum knows the amount of weapons one see from early humans. Saying with such confidence that humans believed x or y back then is just inaccurate. Humans before agriculture were not higher, and there is no conclusive evidence life was easier. If anything they had to spend all day searching for a few wild berries and trying to hunt. We have no conclusive evidence life was less hierarchical in nomad structures. I’m feeling uncomfortable because when he is asked a question he doesn’t show any academic humility and answer with “certainty”, he always seem to have an answer and is a conclusive answer. 

I love this content, but I just want to bring attention to a very important thing: being the most famous or the most viewed doesn’t make you the best or the most accurate. He without a doubt has enormous knowledge that I don’t possess (exactly the reason why I’m listening to his classes) but for certain things (the sociological, cultural and now pre-historical or early historical) paradigms, theories or informed guesses he also likes to make, if done with no academic recognition of some degree of ignorance (humility) are not responsible or accurate.

### **@drogabodiroga3869**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Jjqf9T59uY0&lc=Ugyxcjbv7cqJudmbg954AaABAg)

Why do you claim that the consensus amongst the scholars is that the reason for the transition is religion?
This is simply not true , and I'm really afraid that the whole series is gonna follow it's path based on this.
For example :
Did every early farming communities had dedicated structures for rituals or organized religion? 
How do we know that the opposite is not true? i.e. that farming communities made way to organized religions?
From a simple research , experts have many theories other than religion and they are NOT in any case favoring religion such as :
Climate 
Domestication
Population Pressure 

You are making bold claims but not explaining enough. Having already watched the episode 57 I think you re-iterate it as almost an axiom ,that the main driver of history is religion .

Examples : 
“The Agricultural Revolution was not a sudden event. It took place over centuries and even millennia... It enabled the growth of permanent settlements, larger populations, and eventually, organized religion and government.”
– Harari, Sapiens, 2014, Ch. 5
“Religion and symbolic behavior were present in early farming communities, but it was the farming way of life — with its permanent settlements and surpluses — that allowed for the development of complex religious institutions.”
– Ian Hodder, Religion at Work in a Neolithic Society, 2014
“The establishment of food production was a prerequisite for the appearance of large permanent communities, social stratification, and institutionalized religion.”
– Bellwood, First Farmers, 2005, p. 36
“It was the surplus generated by agriculture that made possible full-time religious specialists, temples, and the complex rituals that characterize organized religion.”
– Trigger, Understanding Early Civilizations, 2003
